// Restore the saved color scheme preference, or
  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.
  //
  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();

  .arxiv-html-header { display: contents; }

  /* The paper controls' icons are blocks, so they sit centred (the pinned JS shows the theme
     icon by making its span a block). */
  .arxiv-html-site-chrome .ds-site-header-tools svg { display: block; width: 18px; height: 18px; flex-shrink: 0; fill: currentColor; }
  /* ar5iv dims every image in dark mode and puts it on a white wash, for paper figures;
     arXiv's own render as they are (the logo on the bar, the funder logos on their white
     chips). Important: ar5iv's dark rules are unlayered, as specific as
     :root:not([data-theme]) img:not(.ltx_no_dark_filter). */
  :is(.ds-site-header, .ds-site-footer) img { filter: none !important; }
  .ds-site-header img { background-color: transparent !important; }

  /* Wide (the theme's 1280px): the theme lays the body out as a grid whose full-width top rows
     were the band's and its sticky reader bar's. The band takes the first and scrolls away; the
     header, the paper's bar (no site navigation, the paper's controls), takes the second and
     stays in view, the one sticky bar, with the anchor clearance that requires (DESIGN-POLICIES).
     The controls show their names. */
  @media (min-width: 1280px) {
    html { scroll-padding-top: 80px; }
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome { display: contents; }

    .arxiv-html-site-chrome > .ds-site-header { grid-area: header; position: sticky; top: 0; z-index: 3; }
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome .tool-icon { display: none; }
  }
  /* Narrow: the header scrolls away, and the controls show icons (names kept for screen
     readers; Why HTML? goes). The theme's contents panel is fixed below where its old sticky
     bar was, and hidden only once the reader has closed it, so until then it is an invisible box
     over the header and the paper: it shows only while open, under the chrome, in its place in
     the page. The paper's footer (build logs, reporting instructions) keeps the text's side
     inset; the theme gives it none, so it met the screen edges. */
  @media (max-width: 1279px) {

    .ltx_page_navbar { position: absolute; top: auto; }
    .arxiv-html-footer { padding-inline: 1rem; }
  }

Department of Mathematics and Computer Science, University of Southern Denmark
What Information Buys in Active Inference
Melih Kandemir
Mustafa Mert Çelikok
Abstract
Information-theoretic exploration is of primary interest in methodological studies of reinforcement learning (RL) and computational cognitive neuroscience, while the established RL theory builds regret guarantees on confidence sets. This situation leaves open the conditions under which the information-driven exploration found in nature is more advantageous than the exploration of the established RL theory. We put the state-of-the-art methods of these two paradigms, Active Inference (AIF) and Bayes-UCBVI, on an equal footing: both act through the same state abstraction of a tabular MDP with
S
S
states, and their greedy forms differ only in the exploration term. We find that AIF’s information gain decays too fast to serve as a confidence width, locking greedy agents that explore by it out of the optimal action. Calibrating the information gain by a square root yields calibrated AIF, which attains regret of order
T
\sqrt{T}
for an exact abstraction, but no better guarantee than Bayes-UCBVI for a fixed reward. When an adversary chooses rewards from the agent’s data, Bayes-UCBVI’s value-specific widths lock out, whereas calibrated AIF pays a factor
S
\sqrt{S}
, which no goal-memoryless agent avoids on some MDP whose start states the adversary also chooses. Calibrated information gain thus acts as a reward-agnostic
ℓ
1
\ell_{1}
confidence set, preferable to value-specific widths when rewards are chosen from the agent’s data, not when they are fixed.
1
Introduction
Humans think in concepts, and each concept is an abstraction that treats many raw sensory states as equivalent. As the token spaces of frontier AI systems inherit such abstractions from human language, the remarkable navigation power of these systems is limited to the human concept space, and nothing in their training lets them form abstractions outside it. To form a new abstraction, an agent must decide which states to treat as equivalent while it acts, leaving the agent with the dual problems of collecting data to estimate the quality of the existing abstraction and acting in a way to find a better abstraction. We study this coupling of exploration and state abstraction with the tools of learning theory. Our agents act in tabular episodic Markov decision processes (MDPs) and observe the state through an abstraction
ϕ
\phi
, which in general makes the observed process non-Markov
(
Starre et al., 2023
)
.
Active Inference (AIF) is a normative account of this coupling. It formalises the enactivist thesis that an agent’s representation cannot be decoupled from its action
(
Varela et al., 1991
;
Hutto and Myin, 2013
)
by letting a single objective drive both its actions and the learning of its world model
(
Friston et al., 2017
)
. Its single objective, the
expected free energy
, scores an action by its expected reward and by the information it is expected to yield. The same inference learns the agent’s world model by predicting the agent’s observations, and this model includes the abstraction, that is, which states the agent treats as equivalent. AIF thus makes information gain the common currency of explorative policy search and of the abstraction of the state space. In its standard formulation, rewards enter the picture as
preferences
, a fixed prior distribution over desired outcomes on which inference conditions and which it does not update.
Reinforcement learning (RL) theory instead derives exploration terms from the estimation error of a value, such as an upper-confidence bonus
(
Auer et al., 2002
;
Azar et al., 2017
)
or a posterior quantile
(
Kaufmann et al., 2012
;
Tiapkin et al., 2022
)
. It selects abstractions by their consequences for value
(
Maillard et al., 2013
;
Ortner et al., 2014
;
Jiang et al., 2015
;
Dong et al., 2019
)
. Control as Inference (CaI)
(
Toussaint, 2009
;
Levine, 2018
)
is known to fail to explore, because it ignores epistemic uncertainty
(
O’Donoghue et al., 2020
)
, which the information-gain term of AIF captures. K-learning
(
O’Donoghue, 2021
)
and VAPOR
(
Tarbouriech et al., 2023
)
add a posterior-uncertainty term to CaI and attain sublinear Bayesian regret, that is, regret averaged over a prior on MDPs. EUBRL
(
Ma and Lee, 2026
)
adds an epistemic-uncertainty variable to CaI and attains high-probability regret on every MDP. No prior study reports such guarantees for AIF, or for CaI without an added exploration term. Motivated by this gap, we investigate the following question.
When is information gain advantageous, and when is it disadvantageous, in terms of regret, compared with confidence sets, for an agent that explores and learns through a state abstraction?
We put the state-of-the-art methods of these two paradigms, AIF and Bayes-UCBVI
(
Tiapkin et al., 2022
)
, on an equal footing. Bayes-UCBVI is minimax-optimal for stage-dependent kernels, and its posterior quantiles act as value-specific confidence widths. Both agents act through the same abstraction and, under a fixed reward function, hold the same Dirichlet posterior over its transitions, and Bayes-UCBVI differs from the greedy variants of AIF only in its exploration term (Section
3.1
). The comparison therefore isolates information from value as the currency of exploration. We also compare AIF with CaI and with UCBVI
(
Azar et al., 2017
)
. Two features of AIF make the question delicate. First, AIF adds information, measured in nats, to reward at an exchange rate, the precision. Which precision, if any, calibrates the two is unspecified by the core theory. Second, when the abstraction is learned, the data that select it are generated by the agent’s exploration through it. Thus, a poor abstraction can steer the agent away from the data that would reveal its error. The three paragraphs below answer the question for a reward function fixed across episodes, episode-specific reward functions that an adaptive adversary chooses from the agent’s data, and an abstraction that the agent selects from a candidate set, and the three parts of Table
1
list the results. We provide all the proofs in the appendix.

Information gain costs regret under a fixed reward function.
When the reward function is fixed across episodes, optimistic agents explore through
ϕ
\phi
at a cost that scales with
X
X
instead of
S
S
. We call an exploration term
valid
if, with high probability, it is at least the amount by which the estimated next-step value falls short of the true one, so that the agent’s values stay optimistic. This shortfall decays with the visit count
N
N
as
N
−
1
/
2
N^{-1/2}
, whereas the information gain about the transition kernel, which AIF calls novelty, decays as
N
−
1
N^{-1}
. With a weight that does not grow with the number of episodes, an agent with a bonus of this order stops playing the optimal action with constant probability (Theorem
5
), and AIF and CaI incur linear regret (Proposition
6
). A transportation inequality shows that the square root of novelty, multiplied by the range of the value function and a logarithmic factor, is a valid width, a frequentist counterpart of the widths of
Lu and Van Roy (2019)
. The resulting greedy agent,
calibrated AIF
, has regret
𝒪
⁡
(
ϵ
rep
​
K
)
+
𝒪
~
​
(
H
3
/
2
​
Γ
ϕ
​
X
​
A
​
T
)
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{\text{rep}}K)+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(H^{3/2}\sqrt{\Gamma_{\phi}XAT})
(Theorem
7
). This bound exceeds that of Bayes-UCBVI’s value-specific quantile by a factor
Γ
ϕ
\sqrt{\Gamma_{\phi}}
, and calibrated information gain therefore does not improve on the bound attainable with visit counts. Under a fixed reward function, information seeking is thus disadvantageous before calibration and brings no advantage after it.
Under adaptive reward functions, calibrated information gain is advantageous over value-specific widths.
When an adaptive adversary chooses the reward functions, which we call goals, from the agent’s data, Bayes-UCBVI’s value-specific quantile is accurate for every fixed goal but not for a goal chosen after the data, which causes Bayes-UCBVI to incur regret
Ω
⁡
(
K
/
ln
⁡
K
)
\Omega(K/\ln K)
(Proposition
26
). In contrast, novelty does not depend on the reward function. Calibrated AIF with a uniform prior is therefore optimistic for every reward function simultaneously, with a width within constants of the radius of an
ℓ
1
\ell_{1}
confidence set
(
Jaksch et al., 2010
)
. Its advantage is thus over value-specific widths, not over
ℓ
1
\ell_{1}
confidence sets, which
Rosenberg and Mansour (2019)
use against adaptive rewards. Theorem
8
determines the price in regret of this simultaneous optimism. On some MDP with
S
S
states whose start states the adversary also chooses, every agent incurs a factor
S
1
/
4
S^{1/4}
more regret than under a fixed goal. Every
goal-memoryless
agent (Definition
3
), which conditions only on the observed transitions and the current goal and breaks ties at the last step by a local rule, incurs a factor
S
\sqrt{S}
on such an MDP, and AIF is such an agent under the last-step conventions of Lemma
30
. Both factors hold up to logarithmic factors. Calibrated AIF with a uniform prior attains the factor
S
\sqrt{S}
, and goals that an oblivious adversary fixes before the first episode cost no extra regret. An agent that uses past goals as data about the transitions, which AIF’s generative model cannot do, halves the exponent of the price.
Learned abstractions need a common target.
When the agent learns its abstraction, value is again the better target. AIF learns
ϕ
\phi
by predicting observations through an emission model, a probabilistic map from abstract states to ground states. Its information gain about the emission makes it prefer a worse action that yields many distinct observations, with regret linear in
K
K
whenever
S
≥
2
​
K
+
2
S\geq 2K+2
(Proposition
39
). Without the decoder, as in joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPA)
(
LeCun, 2022
;
Assran et al., 2023
)
, the agent scores each candidate abstraction by how well it predicts its own labels. This target is self-referential. A coarse abstraction, one that merges states whose best actions differ, predicts its own labels well and is never refuted by the data that it generates. It thus incurs linear regret (Proposition
40
). GOLF
(
Jin et al., 2021
)
avoids this problem, because it uses the value function as a target that all candidate abstractions share. It attains regret
𝒪
~
​
(
T
)
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{T})
in realizable block MDPs with latent rewards. The rate also holds when an adaptive adversary chooses latent goals (Theorem
9
).
Consequently, calibrated information gain acts as a reward-agnostic confidence set of the
ℓ
1
\ell_{1}
type. It is advantageous over value-specific confidence widths when the reward functions are chosen from the agent’s own data, and it is not when the reward function is fixed. The first case also covers an agent that sets its own goals from its data, such as a homeostatic agent whose set points adapt to its trajectory
(
Sterling, 2012
;
Pezzulo et al., 2015
)
or an autotelic agent
(
Colas et al., 2022
)
. Calibrated AIF with a uniform prior stays optimistic whatever rule sets the goals, whereas Bayes-UCBVI locks out under a rule that uses only the agent’s own data. When the agent learns its abstraction, information about the emission of observations misleads its exploration, and value is the better target.
2

Goals
Section

1
k

, chosen by an

. The goals are said to be

if the adversary chooses them from the interaction history of the agent, and

if it fixes all goals and start states before the first episode. We call the adversary adaptive or oblivious accordingly. The regret against these goals is

.
The

of a triple
(

,

,

)
(h,x,a)

Definition 0
(Goal-memoryless agents)
.
An agent is
goal-memoryless
if (i) its policy in each episode is a function of the records of all triples, of the current goal and of randomness drawn afresh for the episode, and (ii) at the last step, at every state
x
x
at which all actions have the same reward under the current goal, it draws its action by a fixed rule from the records of the triples
(
H
,
x
,
⋅
)
(H,x,\cdot)
and from
U
x
,
j
U_{x,j}
. Here
j
j
counts its earlier last-step visits to
x
x
, and the
U
x
,
j
U_{x,j}
are independent uniform variables, independent of everything else. Fixed parameters, such as
K
K
and
δ
\delta
, may enter both rules.
Condition (ii) excludes the goal, the records of other states and the episode index from these choices. Otherwise the agent’s actions at the last step could record the order of its earlier, goal-dependent choices (Appendix
G.3
). A goal-memoryless agent conditions on the current goal but does not use past goals as data about the transitions. Bayes-UCBVI, CaI, AIF and calibrated AIF (Sections
3.3
and
4
) are goal-memoryless if, at the last step, the greedy agents break ties by a fixed order of the actions and AIF and calibrated AIF with Bayes-UCBVI’s prior act on their index before the cap at
r
0
r_{0}
(Lemma
30
in Appendix
G.3
). UCBVI is not goal-memoryless, since it clamps its values at those of the previous episode, which were computed under earlier goals. Condition (ii) constrains only the last step at states where all actions earn the same reward, and the agents above meet it with a fixed tie order or with randomness drawn by local visit. Neither our upper bound nor our lock-out argument covers UCBVI under adaptive goals.

Value-specific widths are not even optimistic for all goals at once, and Bayes-UCBVI’s quantiles lock out under adaptive goals (Proposition

). A value-specific quantile is accurate for every fixed goal but not for a data-dependent goal, whereas the confidence set behind

certifies whatever value the agent computes. In Proposition

Goals as data.
AIF treats goals as preferences, on which it conditions but which it does not update. It therefore does not use past goals as data, and part (b) applies to it and to its calibrated variant (Lemma
30
). By part (c), calibrated AIF with a uniform prior attains the rate of part (b) in
S
S
and
K
K
. Calibration is thus what makes information gain useful under adaptive goals, and no goal-memoryless agent does better. The remaining gap between parts (a) and (b) is the value of using past goals as data. A goal chosen by an adversary that knows the transition law is an observation of that law, and AIF’s generative model has no variable for it.

AIF predicts the next ground state by posterior averaging over the candidate abstractions, a consistent score (Proposition

), but its information gain about the emission directs its exploration (Appendix

). Given the decoder, the latent kernel and the rewards of a block MDP with

of its own labels, and a

). Every latent-predictive agent therefore incurs regret

6
Conclusion
For a fixed reward function, raw information gain of vanilla AIF incurs linear regret, and calibrated information gain does not improve on the bound attainable with visit counts. Under adaptive goals, calibrated information gain makes the agent optimistic for every goal at once, as an
ℓ
1
\ell_{1}
confidence set does, whereas the value-specific widths of Bayes-UCBVI lock out with regret
Ω
⁡
(
K
/
ln
⁡
K
)
\Omega(K/\ln K)
. The price of adaptive goals is a factor
S
1
/
4
S^{1/4}
for every agent and
S
\sqrt{S}
for goal-memoryless agents such as AIF, up to logarithmic factors. For learned abstractions, predicting observations or the abstraction’s own labels misleads exploration and selection, whereas the value function is a target that all candidate abstractions share. Calibrated information gain is therefore advantageous over value-specific confidence widths when the goals are chosen from the agent’s own data, and not when the reward function is fixed. Open questions are whether calibrated AIF with Bayes-UCBVI’s pseudo-state prior is optimistic for every goal, whether the price of adaptive goals holds with a fixed start state and for every horizon, whether it holds on every MDP rather than in expectation over a prior, and whether a single agent that uses past goals as data attains the factor
S
1
/
4
S^{1/4}
without knowing the adversary’s strategy. A further open problem concerns an agent that moves its own goals towards the attractors into which the MDP and its exploration rule drift it. Goals and policy then form a coupled dynamical system, and it is unknown whether this system converges and to which attractors. Whether physiological set points are defended targets or settling points of such coupled dynamics is itself debated in mathematical biology
(
Mrosovsky, 1990
;
Speakman et al., 2011
)
. A two-time-scale analysis
(
Borkar, 1997
)
with goals adapting slower than the policy is a natural starting point.
7
AI Disclosure
The discovery of the untapped problem in the way it stands in the main question box in the introduction belongs completely to the authors. All the most creative moments of the investigation process have also been due to the deep and to-the-point involvement of the authors with their both broad field knowledge and their nuanced intuitions. That being said, the authors used frontier AI technologies in all steps of the work, sometimes as a companion and other times as the main workhorse. The eventual outcome benefited substantially from AI support, while being an original product of the authors.
The overall structure of the paper is engineered in detail by the authors and AI is used across several tens of times to revise the whole text end-to-end based on lengthy prompts created by the authors after their careful critical reading. These prompts contained structural improvements, new literature elements to be checked, new perspectives to be put on some of the existing content, and new concept pairs to be connected. Nearly all the abstract is the original text of the authors. The paragraph-level design of the introduction and a substantial amount of the text contained therein are native to the authors. The authors provided the AI agents a long list of papers known well to them, pointing to their precise pieces of content that are relevant for the work to be done. The follow-up literature survey text is then left to AI generation, though its post-hoc fact checking is done first by the authors and by alternative AI models afterwards.
The approaching angle to the problem, for example putting AIF and Bayes-UCBVI into the equal footing by defining an abstract MDP, belongs completely to the authors. Similarly, the authors gave several rounds of long instructions to the AI agents about what sort of theoretical claims to be sought and which proof techniques may lead to a successful solution. For example, a verbal (though coarse) description of Theorem 1 was provided to the AI agent as a very likely outcome expected from putting together certain pieces of a particular list of existing work. The counter-examples found for regret lower bounds came from AI agents, despite after long interactions where many intermediate strategic decisions were made by the authors based on their scrutiny and discussion. The idea of looking into adaptive goals emerged as a long and argumentative interaction of the first author with Claude Opus 5.5 high effort, where both sides made creative contributions to the final decision of investigating that realm.
The authors used multiple AI agents in different roles at different stages of the work, that span Claude Opus 5.5 medium, high, and max effort levels mainly for content-related matters, GPT Sol 6.1 for auditing, and GLM 5.3 for narrowly defined text manipulation tasks. The authors made tedious and repeated checks on the whole spectrum of the final product and to the best of their efforts, the claims are sound.
This very AI disclosure text is written verbatim by the authors without any computer involvement whatsoever, including typo finding or text polishing, in order to set a clear reference for their own writing style. Potential grammatical or typographical errors should be attributed to this fact.

Table
3
positions our contribution among the closest analyses, and the footnotes of Table
1
name the results we build on. Information-gain bonuses
(

)
and ensemble-disagreement bonuses
(

)

5

Adaptive goals and adversarial MDPs.
With unknown transitions and full-information rewards,
ℓ
1
\ell_{1}
confidence sets attain
𝒪
~
​
(
H
​
S
​
A
​
K
)
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(HS\sqrt{AK})
against adaptive reward sequences
(
Rosenberg and Mansour, 2019
)
, as
Tiapkin et al. (2025, §3.2)
note, and policy optimisation attains
poly
⁡
(
H
)
​
S
​
A
​
T
\mathrm{poly}(H)\sqrt{SAT}
against oblivious ones
(
Tiapkin et al., 2025
)
. These works compare with the best fixed policy and reveal each reward after its episode. We instead reveal each goal before its episode and compare with each goal’s optimum. Neither setting therefore contains the other. Our protocol is that of adversarial contextual MDPs
(
Modi and Tewari, 2020
;
Levy et al., 2023
)
, with the goal as the context and transitions that do not depend on it. These works learn context-dependent transitions and rewards, whereas our agents are given the goal, and our lower bounds rest on an adversary that chooses it from the agent’s samples. Reward-free exploration needs a number of episodes quadratic in
S
S
for arbitrary rewards
(
Jin et al., 2020
;
Ridel and Peled-Cohen, 2026
)
, but only linear in
S
S
for polynomially many rewards fixed in advance
(
Li et al., 2024
)
. Theorem
8
(b) and Proposition
38
are the online counterparts of these two rates. For policy optimisation against adversarial rewards, closing the remaining gaps is open even in the worst case
(
Li et al., 2026a
)
.
Adaptive data analysis.
The goals of Theorem
8
(b) are aligned with the agent’s sampling error, which makes its estimates biased exactly along the values it is asked to compute. This is the false-discovery problem of adaptive data analysis
(
Dwork et al., 2015
;
Hardt and Ullman, 2014
;
Steinke and Ullman, 2015
)
, with one difference. There, the analyst sees only the answers, and noise added to them restores validity. Our adversary sees the samples. Hence, no noise added to the agent’s answers helps.
Self-predictive representations.
Joint-embedding predictive architectures
(
LeCun, 2022
;
Assran et al., 2023
)
and self-predictive RL
(
Gelada et al., 2019
;
Schwarzer et al., 2021
)
learn a representation by predicting its own next value rather than the next observation.
Ni et al. (2024)
relate latent self-prediction, together with reward prediction, to model-irrelevance abstractions through a condition on the full state or history. Proposition
40
shows that selecting among candidates by the likelihood of their own labels can favour, under every policy, a candidate that merges two states whose best actions differ, and that the labels of such a candidate cannot refute it when the agent acts through it. GOLF’s Bellman residual is also a self-prediction, but it compares each function with the best predictor of the same target. Selecting an abstraction by its consequences for value relates to the value-equivalence principle, which learns a model only as far as it matters for value
(
Farahmand et al., 2017
;
Grimm et al., 2020
;
Arumugam and Van Roy, 2022
)
. Representation selection with regret guarantees incurs a factor
|
Φ
|
\sqrt{|\Phi|}
through
hard selection
, an
arg
⁡
max
\arg\max
or an elimination test
(
Maillard et al., 2011
;
Maillard et al., 2013
;
Ortner et al., 2014
)
, whereas Theorem
9
incurs
ln
⁡
|
Φ
|
\sqrt{\ln|\Phi|}
in block MDPs with latent rewards.
State abstractions, unlike the temporal abstractions of hierarchical RL
(
Sutton et al., 1999
;
Bacon et al., 2017
)
, are computed from a known model
(
Givan et al., 2003
;
Li et al., 2006
)
, learned from tasks
(
Abel et al., 2018
)
, or selected from data
(
Jiang et al., 2015
)
. The regret of a fixed aggregation is bounded from above by
Dong et al. (2019)
. Data observed through an abstraction call for martingale concentration
(
Starre et al., 2023
)
. Work on rich-observation RL learns the decoder of a block MDP while exploring, which is the realizable case of our setting
(
Du et al., 2019
;
Misra et al., 2020
;
Agarwal et al., 2020
;
Efroni et al., 2022

)
. The case

. The

Millidge et al. (2020)

Several results connect to earlier work beyond Table

Appendix G
Proofs for Section
4
We prove the results of Section
4
in the order of their dependencies. Appendix
G.1
shows that calibrated AIF with a uniform prior is optimistic for every goal at once and bounds its regret, the first claim of Theorem
8
(c). Appendix
G.2
proves that Bayes-UCBVI’s value-specific quantiles lock out under adaptive goals (Proposition
26
), and that every index that is optimistic for all goals at once overestimates some goal (Lemma

). Its

argument underlies the lower bound for goal-memoryless agents, Theorem
8
(b), in Appendix
G.3
, which also shows which agents are goal-memoryless. Appendix
G.4
collects the concentration tools of the lower bound for every agent, Theorem
8
(a), which Appendix
G.5
proves. Appendix
G.6
gives agents that use past goals as data and attain this rate, Proposition
37
and the second claim of Theorem
8
(c). Appendix
G.7
treats goals fixed in advance, Theorem
8
(d) and Proposition
38
.

Scope of Proposition
26
.
Its construction uses that Bayes-UCBVI adds no width at the last step, where its next-step values vanish. UCBVI adds its bonus at every step, including the last, where the successors’ own counts, of order

exhibits a goal chosen after the data that it underestimates.
\jmlrQED

One agent for all adversaries.
The agent above depends on the adversary, and we now remove this dependence with the minimax theorem. Let every agent round each revealed reward down to the grid

, rounding changes the value of each action by at most
1
/
K
2
1/K^{2}
, and hence the regret of every agent against every adversary by at most
2
/
K
2/K
in total. Consider the game in which the adversary chooses rounded goals and start states, the agent chooses actions, chance draws
σ
\sigma
from the prior and the successors, and the agent pays its expected regret against the rounded goals. Each player chooses among finitely many options at each of finitely many decision points. Both players thus have finitely many pure strategies, and the expected regret is bilinear in their mixed strategies. Von Neumann’s minimax theorem therefore gives
min

⁡
max

​

over mixed strategies. A mixed adversary draws a pure strategy at the start and is a randomised adversary, against which the Thompson-sampling agent that knows it attains the bound above. The right-hand side is therefore at most this bound, and a minimising mixed agent on the left attains it against every pure adversary, and hence against every mixture of them. Every adversary of the original interaction, composed with the rounding, is such a mixture, with its own randomness and its unrounded choices as private randomness. This agent therefore has regret at most the bound above plus

≤

2/K\leq 1
against every adversary.

AIF’s Selection by Observations
This subsection states Propositions

and

Predicting observations.
AIF predicts the next ground state by
posterior averaging
, as it averages over policies by Bayesian model averaging
(
Friston et al., 2017
)
. Each candidate composes a latent transition with an emission, and AIF weights the candidates’ Bayesian predictions by their posterior probabilities (Appendix
D.2
). All candidates predict the same observations, which makes this score consistent. On every trajectory, posterior averaging predicts the next ground state as well as the Bayesian prediction of
ϕ
†
\phi^{\dagger}
, up to

⁡
|
Φ

\ln|\Phi|

(

(Proposition

). The price is that AIF’s information gain about the emission directs its exploration.

Predicting the abstraction’s own state.
Proposition
39
suggests removing the emission model. Joint-embedding predictive architectures
(

and self-predictive representations in RL

predict the next abstract state rather than the next observation. The score
Λ

of Section

is AIF’s model of Appendix

without its emission factor. It has no emission, and hence no information gain about an emission. Removing the emission, however, also changes the target. Each candidate abstraction is now scored by how well it predicts its own labels, a target that differs from one candidate abstraction to the next. We now show that this self-referential target can confirm a coarse abstraction, one that merges two states whose best actions differ, whatever rule the agent uses to explore.
